## 1. Grammar for OET: Verb Tenses

- Lesson ID and folder: `grammar-01-verb-tenses` (folder `grammar-01-verb-tenses`); course grammar, lesson 1; length 129:04; bundle format 1.23
- Status: approved by Siavash, 2026-10-31 10:33
- Description: Learn the verb tenses, when to use each one, and how to use them in your OET writing.
- Original session: folder `source`: `source/video.mp4`, 125:10 long, partial hash `8fd03776aa09bf2b`; deck `source/slides.pdf`, 19 pages

*Rules on the boards*

- 9:49 A perfect form connects an action or a state to a later point in time: up to now, or up to a point in the past. It does not mean the action is finished.
- 19:26 Finished in the past: simple past. Started in the past and still true now: present perfect. A fact about now: present simple.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 13:03 'Before admission' is a fixed point in the past. For this sentence choose the simple past or the past perfect, not the present perfect.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 7:15 The patient will smokes tomorrow.
- 8:53 You was smoking.
- 11:42 Had wrote.
- 12:20 The patient will has.

*Terms*: Continuous (Form, 2:45); Perfect (Form, 3:05); Perfect continuous (Form, 3:24); Helping verb (New term, 3:47); Subject and verb (New term, 5:15); Regular verb (New term, 5:44); Infinitive (New term, 6:43); The -ing form (Form, 8:21); Past participle (New term, 11:09); Time markers (Time words, 13:15); Perfect continuous (Form, 14:39); been (Form, 15:25); experience (New word, 17:12)

*Rules on the boards*

- 20:41 You choose the verb tense. Choose the tense that matches the meaning you want.
- 23:15 For a plan at a set time, you can use the present simple instead of 'will'.
- 25:27 With one time in the past and nothing earlier, use the past simple.
- 27:41 Use 'will' for a prediction, for a decision you make now, and after 'I hope'.

#### 27:58 Usage: continuous tenses (`page-8`, deck)

- 27:58 **the three model sentences, the present column, the past column, the future column**

*Rules on the boards*

- 28:52 In all three sentences the action is in progress at a point in time. Only the point moves.
- 31:48 The present continuous does not need a time marker: the tense already tells us the time is now. Add 'right now' only to stress this exact moment.
- 34:50 The patient was smoking is not wrong grammar, but the meaning is not complete. The time marker completes it.
- 36:18 The past continuous and the future continuous need a time marker. The present continuous does not.

*Terms*: Continuous tense (New term, 28:38); Persistent (New word, 30:26); Time marker (Time words, 34:20)

*Rules on the boards*

- 38:03 A perfect form does not by itself tell you the action is finished.
- 40:31 When you use a time marker, your tense must match it. 'For' and 'since' often go with the present perfect. They also go with the past perfect.
- 42:57 Without 'before discharge tomorrow' the sentence is still grammatical, but the meaning is not complete. Keep the time marker.
- 43:54 Choose the tense from the meaning you want and from the time markers in the sentence.

*Terms*: Perfect tenses (Grammar term, 36:59); For and since (Time words, 39:20); Time marker (Grammar term, 40:09)

*Rules on the boards*

- 59:05 'For' gives the length of the period. 'Since' gives the point when it started.
- 60:36 The end of the period decides the tense. A finished period takes the simple past. A period up to now takes the present perfect.
- 63:06 Use the simple past with 'ago'.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 62:01 Writing 'from 2010' with the present perfect is a mistake. Write 'since 2010'.
- 65:05 Do not use 'at' with a year or a month. We say 'in 2004', not 'at 2004'.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 61:12 The patient has had hypertension from 2010

*Terms*: Time markers (New term, 57:32); Receive treatment (New phrase, 59:44); Passive (New term, 63:51)

  - 94:38 Reassure about what the exam asks for
- 94:50 **row 3, the appointment in the present simple**
  - 94:50 Explain the present simple in a sentence about tomorrow
  - 95:12 Draw the time: the appointment exists now, the visit is tomorrow
  - 95:22 Show that the will-form is also correct and why we choose the present simple
- 95:43 **row 3 passive, will be seen**
  - 95:43 Explain why the passive here is future
  - 95:58 Name the participle and the doer
- 96:11 **row 3, focus on the appointment and the verb schedule**
  - 96:11 Teach the verb 'schedule' in English
  - 96:31 Show the active and passive present perfect side by side
  - 96:52 Name the three parts of the present perfect passive
- 97:28 **the tenses used most in a letter**
  - 97:28 Frame what a letter reports and name the first tense
  - 97:39 Name the second tense
  - 97:46 Draw the present perfect time reference
  - 97:55 State the rule to remember and reassure about the forms

*Rules on the boards*

- 87:08 In the passive the object moves to the front. The doer is often left out; you can add it with 'by'. The verb changes form too.
- 90:26 The -ed on a past participle is not the past tense -ed. In every passive the main verb is a past participle.
- 97:55 Most sentences in a letter use the simple past or the present perfect.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 93:18 Arthritis was had by the patient.

*Terms*: Passive (Grammar term, 84:54); Subject (Grammar term, 86:06); Object (Grammar term, 86:12); Active (Grammar term, 86:16); Be verb plus past participle (Form, 88:58); Past participle (Grammar term, 89:16); Schedule (New word, 96:18); Has been scheduled (Form, 96:52)

#### 98:10 Passive forms of the verb tenses (`page-16`, deck)

- 98:10 **what the table shows and the four groups**
  - 98:10 Set up the board: one sentence in the passive across all 12 tenses, grouped in four families of form
  - 98:28 Define the grammar term 'passive' the first time it appears
  - 98:57 Give the study priority for the whole table
  - 99:09 Fix the priority as a rule to remember
- 99:31 **the simple passive forms**
  - 99:31 the three simple passive rows of the table
  - 100:05 Define past participle, the part of the passive that never changes
  - 100:24 State what actually changes between the three rows
  - 100:40 Note the plural subject change
  - 100:57 Replace the first-language glosses with an English time reference for the three forms
- 101:20 **the continuous passive forms**
  - 101:20 the two continuous passive rows
  - 101:40 State how the continuous passive is built
  - 101:56 Contrast the simple and continuous passive to show what 'being' adds
- 102:13 **the perfect passive forms**
  - 102:13 the two useful perfect passive rows
  - 102:33 State how the perfect passive is built
  - 102:45 Mark these two forms as worth learning
  - 102:53 Set the future perfect passive aside
- 103:08 **the passive forms we do not use**
  - 103:08 the rejected rows of the table

  - 104:47 Say where the future form is needed
  - 105:01 Close with the study rule for the whole table

*Rules on the boards*

- 99:09 Learn the simple forms well.
- 103:42 You do not need these forms. Learn the simple and perfect passives instead.
- 105:01 Practise these five forms until you can use them easily.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 103:08 OxyContin will be being given to control the pain.
- 103:08 OxyContin had been being given to control the pain.
- 103:08 OxyContin has been being given to control the pain.

*Terms*: Passive (Grammar term, 98:28); Past participle (Grammar term, 100:05)

*Rules on the boards*

- 111:48 We choose the passive when the doer is not known, or is obvious, or is not the point.
- 114:40 Look at the meaning of the verb and its subject. That shows you which form to use.

*Rules on the boards*

- 116:13 In the letter you cannot write in note form. Add a verb, choose the right tense, and add a subject where you need one.
- 120:16 Verbs like 'diagnose' happen at one time and finish. Use the simple past with them.
- 127:27 A date that marks one finished time, like this one, takes a past tense. With 'since' and a date, use the present perfect.
- 128:48 Both parts of the sentence talk about the same past date, so both verbs are in the past.

*Rules on the boards*

- 13:33 Verbs like start, begin, initiate, commence and finish name one point in time. With a finished date, use the simple past.
- 15:25 When in, on or at points to a date in the past, use the simple past.
- 16:02 Small words are easy to forget when you write quickly. A missing a is marked as an error.
- 18:42 6 months is a length of time. Since does not go with a length of time.
- 31:30 Use the simple past for a finding taken on a particular date. There is no progression here.
- 34:07 Unless the case notes say the symptom has gone, use the simple present for symptoms that have not changed.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 17:22 From is not a time word here. Do not write from 5 years or from 2013.
- 29:00 Use a comma only where you are sure. A wrong comma counts as a grammar mistake.
- 32:13 If you are not sure the reader will know a short form, write it in full: 'coronary artery disease', not 'CAD'.

*Rules on the boards*

- 18:35 A countable noun that is singular and new to the reader needs a or an.
- 20:46 Use "an" before a vowel sound: an abscess. Use "a" before a consonant sound. Follow the sound, not the letter.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 19:50 When the age comes before the noun, write hyphens: 15-year-old. Without them it is a writing error.
- 23:06 Students often leave out the article with "allergy" and "ache".

- Description: Learn how to join two ideas in one complex sentence, with the right linking word and comma, for your OET letters.

#### 2:13 What is a complex sentence? (`pages-04-05`, deck)

- 2:13 **Why complex sentences, and the two kinds of clause**
  - 2:13 Set out the slide's point: medical situations are complex, so your sentences must be too
  - 2:44 Link the point to the OET task: paraphrasing case notes
  - 3:00 Separate compound from complex and name the topic of the lesson
  - 3:14 Show the score consequence: simple sentences only means grade C
  - 3:57 Define clause and independent clause
  - 4:34 Define dependent clause and contrast the two kinds
- 5:12 **Complex sentence diagram: Although Karen discontinued...**
  - 5:12 Reveal the slide's clause diagram, piece by piece, and gloss the hard word
  - 5:57 Name what the diagram shows and the shared structure of both clauses
  - 6:23 Stand-alone test on the second clause: it is independent
  - 6:54 Stand-alone test on the first clause: it is dependent

  - 8:00 Name the joining word in the example
  - 8:06 State what conjunctions are used for
  - 8:18 Show the finished complex sentence

*Rules on the boards*

- 3:14 If you write everything in simple sentences, the links between the facts are lost.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 7:24 A dependent clause with a full stop is not a sentence. In a letter, never write "Although she has good family support." on its own. Join it to an independent clause.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 6:54 Although Karen discontinued her use of Microgynon 30.

*Terms*: Clause (Grammar term, 3:57); Independent clause (Grammar term, 4:15); Dependent clause (Grammar term, 4:34); Subordinate clause (Grammar term, 7:41); Conjunction (Grammar term, 8:00)

#### 9:49 From case notes to a sentence (`page-6`, deck)

- 9:49 **Case notes turned into one complex sentence**
  - 9:49 Show the notes and the finished sentence, and frame the task

  - 10:50 Read the last two notes: the family comment and the action taken

  - 13:03 Define dependent clause before the diagram
  - 13:09 Define independent clause before the diagram
  - 13:30 Draw the sentence as two clause pieces joined by 'Although'
  - 14:14 Sum up what the sentence is made of

*Rules on the boards*

- 11:17 When two notes contrast like this, join them in one sentence instead of listing them one by one.
- 12:43 'Due to his high care needs' is not a clause. It has no subject and no verb. It is a phrase.

*Terms*: Hoist (New word, 10:34); Although (Joining word, 11:48); Due to (Linking words, 11:55); Dependent clause (Grammar, 13:03); Independent clause (Grammar, 13:09)

#### 15:44 Subordinating conjunctions (`page-7`, deck)

- 15:44 **The joining words**
  - 15:44 Show the list of joining words and recall the two clauses of a complex sentence
  - 15:44 Show both orders as clause diagrams and teach order 1
  - 16:33 Name the glue and its two types
  - 16:55 Contrast subordinating and coordinating conjunctions
  - 17:33 Memory aid: the subordinate clause and its conjunction
  - 18:21 What each joining word shows

  - 19:18 Who for people, which for things, and the comma

  - 21:42 Explain order 1 and then show order 2
  - 22:14 Explain order 2 and its comma
  - 22:36 Warn against the missing comma

*Rules on the boards*

- 17:54 A subordinating conjunction starts the subordinate clause. That is the glue for complex sentences.
- 19:33 When who or which only adds extra information, put a comma before it, and another after that information if the sentence goes on.
- 22:44 When the dependent clause comes first, put a comma after it. It shows the reader where the first clause ends.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 22:36 After she started the new medication the patient felt better.

*Terms*: Conjunction (Term, 16:33); Subordinate clause (Term, 17:33); Relative clause (Term, 19:03)

#### 23:54 Compound or complex? (`pages-08-09`, deck)

- 23:54 **Compound and complex sentence boxes**
  - 23:54 Open the two boxes and name the joining word of a compound sentence
  - 24:24 Teach the seven coordinating conjunctions through FANBOYS
  - 24:50 State the FANBOYS test as the rule to remember
  - 24:59 Both sentence types are built from two clauses

  - 26:51 State what the diagram shows

  - 27:40 Give the correct version of the comma splice

  - 30:10 Set up the test printed on the slide and reject FANBOYS inside a complex sentence
  - 30:27 Warn what the wrong joining word does to the sentence
  - 30:44 Re-express the slide's 'glue' gloss in English and give the test
  - 31:01 State the complement of the test: anything else is subordinating
  - 31:26 Reject two joining words in one sentence: Although ... but ...
  - 31:56 Give the correct version and the rule behind it
  - 32:12 Same rule with Because ... so ...

*Rules on the boards*

- 24:50 When one of these seven words joins the two parts, the sentence is compound.
- 31:01 A subordinating conjunction, like because or although, makes a complex sentence.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 30:27 The wrong joining word can change the meaning of your sentence, or make it hard for the reader to understand. Then the sentence is wrong.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 25:35 He examined immediately.
- 27:21 He presented yesterday, he was examined immediately.
- 31:26 Although her pain has reduced, but she insists on taking the medication.
- 32:12 Because the medication causes drowsiness, so do not drive.

*Terms*: Coordinating conjunction (Form, 24:15); FANBOYS (Memory word, 24:24); Clause (Term, 25:04); Independent clause (Term, 25:12); Passive (Term, 25:58); Subordinating conjunction (Form, 27:58); Dependent clause (Term, 28:07); Conjunction (Term, 30:44)

  - 50:11 reducing the time clause
  - 50:42 the version that breaks the same-subject rule
  - 52:20 how much to reduce

*Rules on the boards*

- 46:45 You must see quickly whether the verb is active or passive. Review it in the lesson Verb Tenses.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 42:06 Concerning about lower back pain
- 44:29 Due to being a recently qualified teacher, her professional voice use may have contributed to the dysphonia.
- 50:42 Being keen to return to work, I suggested she practise affirmations and meditations when feeling anxious.

*Terms*: Present participle (Form, 33:19); Past participle (Form, 33:19); Perfect participle (Form, 33:19); Participle clause (Grammar term, 33:21); Dangling participle (Grammar term, 37:18); Be + -ed adjective (Grammar term, 41:38)

#### 52:37 Expressing reason with 'given' (`page-11`, deck)

- 52:37 **Given: case notes table**

  - 53:02 show the meaning inside the slide's own example
  - 53:16 correct what 'given' expresses

*Rules on the boards*

- 53:16 'Given' gives a reason. It can also give a condition that is already true: given that her kidney function is normal, the dose can stay the same.
- 57:36 'Given' joins two case notes into one sentence. It is useful in the closing part of your letter.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 55:54 Given his age, Mr X is in excellent physical condition.

*Terms*: given (New word, 52:54); given that (Form, 54:21); despite (Word for a contrast, 56:10)

## 7. Grammar for OET: Punctuation

- Lesson ID and folder: `grammar-07-punctuation` (folder `grammar-07-punctuation`); course grammar, lesson 7; length 95:45; bundle format 1.23
- Status: approved by siavash, 2026-10-27 09:37
- Description: Learn to punctuate your OET letters: where to put commas, and how to use full stops, semicolons, colons, apostrophes, hyphens, capital letters, numbers and short forms correctly.
- Original session: folder `source`: `source/video.mp4`, 33:26 long, partial hash `297821a41d0a0437`; deck `source/slides.pdf`, 18 pages
  - The recording teaches commas only; the second part (sections added-01 to added-09) is authored, with no recording (ADR 018).
- Parts: Commas in OET letters; Other punctuation in OET letters

*Rules on the boards*

- 3:48 Using compound and complex sentences shows a range of sentence types in your letter.
- 4:37 Separate an introductory or transitional phrase from the rest of the sentence with a comma.

  - 10:43 The correct preposition

*Rules on the boards*

- 13:03 In a complex sentence, when the dependent clause comes first, put a comma after it, before the independent clause.

  - 14:29 Reveal the definition and the slide example, and define 'independent clause'

  - 16:09 Draw the example as two complete pieces joined by 'so'
  - 16:47 Where the comma goes, and the rule to remember

  - 17:40 Why join clauses instead of writing two sentences

*Rules on the boards*

- 17:01 To join two independent clauses with a comma, you also need a FANBOYS word. A comma alone is not enough.
- 21:03 Each pause is one punctuation mark: the semi-colon, then the comma.
- 23:01 When a comma joins two independent clauses, check that a FANBOYS word is there too.

- 21:44 Eye drops were administered, the patient was told to return several days later.

  - 25:41 Split the third example into phrase and main clause
  - 25:49 Generalise the rule across all three examples
  - 26:18 Close with the rule to remember

*Rules on the boards*

- 25:49 The part after the comma is a main clause: it works as a complete sentence on its own. The part in front of the comma is the introductory phrase.
- 26:18 When a sentence starts with an introductory phrase, put a comma after it.

*Terms*: Introductory phrase (New term, 23:16); Main clause (independent clause) (Grammar term, 23:57)

*Rules on the boards*

- 25:55 An OET letter is 180 to 200 words. Do not add extra words to reach it: the information you must give will fill it.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 18:53 Extra information slows the reader down and makes it harder to find the key points. It also costs you marks.
- 26:10 Extra words can push you over 200 words and hide the key points.

*Terms*: Omit (Technique 2, 17:51); a fifty-year-old teacher (Form, 23:00); a social drinker (New phrase, 25:22)

*Rules on the boards*

- 4:45 When 'and' joins two words like these, they are usually the same kind of word: two nouns, or two adjectives.

*Rules on the boards*

- 42:21 Extra information is separated with a comma.
- 44:18 A defining relative clause takes no comma.
- 47:05 When you use a relative pronoun, delete the word it replaces: she, it or this.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 44:27 Look at the words before who or whose. After a description with 'a' (a 55-year-old bus driver whose …), no comma. Straight after the patient's name (Ms Wilson, who …), a comma.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 37:06 He lives with his 80-year-old mother. She suffers from dementia.
- 37:06 She tried a herbal remedy. She obtained it over the counter from a Chinese shop.
- 37:06 Ms Wilson is a 55-year-old bus driver. Her eyesight may be deteriorating.
- 38:14 He suffered a severe blow to the head. This caused him to lose consciousness.
- 38:14 He reported no feelings of faintness or dizziness. I found this surprising.
- 38:14 I spent some time counselling Mr Aksoy. His mother died last week.
- 38:50 He lives with his 80-year-old mother. She suffers from dementia.
- 41:07 She tried a herbal remedy. She obtained it over the counter from a Chinese shop.
- 41:55 Which she obtained it over the counter from a Chinese shop.
- 42:28 Ms Wilson is a 55-year-old bus driver. Her eyesight may be deteriorating.
- 44:51 He suffered a severe blow to the head. This caused him to lose consciousness.
- 45:50 He suffered from a severe blow to the head.
- 45:57 He suffered from a leg fracture.
- 46:21 He reported no feelings of faintness or dizziness. I found this surprising.
- 47:15 I spent some time counselling Mr Aksoy. His mother died last week.

*Terms*: Relative pronoun (New term, 38:59); Relative clause (New term, 39:10); Who, which, whose, where (Relative pronouns, 40:53); Herbal (New word, 41:15); Remedy (New word, 41:18); Obtain (New word, 41:26); Over the counter (New phrase, 41:29); Deteriorate (Word family, 43:07); Her, his, their (Possessive words, 43:18); Blow (New word, 44:58); Lose consciousness (New phrase, 45:01); Faint (Word family, 46:41); Counsel (Word family, 47:50)

*Rules on the boards*

- 55:32 When the two sentences join with 'which', the word it replaces ('them', 'this') is dropped.
- 58:07 Do not leave the preposition out when you join the sentences. Here we used 'from', 'for' and 'by'.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 50:09 Most learners leave the preposition out. The sentence is then wrong, and the meaning is not clear.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 48:21 He suffered a stroke last January. He has still not fully recovered from it.
- 48:21 She occasionally suffers from constipation. She takes a mild laxative for this.
- 48:21 He was advised to rest the ankle for two weeks. Instead of this, he went on a walking holiday.
- 49:26 She tried two different herbal remedies. Neither of them had any effect.
- 49:26 He presented again on 23 January. By this time his condition had worsened.
- 49:26 He has two moles on his left arm. One of them appears to have changed shape.
- 50:03 He suffered a stroke last January. He has still not fully recovered from it.
- 50:46 He suffered a stroke last January, which he has still not fully recovered from it.
- 52:09 She occasionally suffers from constipation. She takes a mild laxative for this.
- 52:35 She occasionally suffers from constipation which she takes a mild laxative for this.
- 53:48 He was advised to rest the ankle for two weeks. Instead of this, he went on a walking holiday.
- 54:52 She tried two different herbal remedies. Neither of them had any effect.
- 56:11 He presented again on 23 January. By this time his condition had worsened.
- 57:12 He has two moles on his left arm. One of them appears to have changed shape.

*Terms*: relative pronoun (Grammar word, 50:19); from which (Form, 51:31); from which, for which, in which (Forms, 53:09); instead of which (Form, 54:21); neither of which (Form, 55:14); one of which (Form, 57:33)

*Rules on the boards*

- 62:56 Hypertension and indigestion are different conditions, so here it is better to keep two sentences.
- 65:55 The next sentence begins with 'However', so we leave these two sentences apart.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 58:54 Join two sentences only when the ideas are related. Sport and a medical history do not belong in one sentence.

*Rules on the boards*

- 72:56 If a sentence is hard to follow, often because it has several ideas or more than about 25 words, split it. Each new sentence needs its own subject and verb.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 74:54 Avoid a chain of relative clauses in one sentence. Change the sentence so you can delete the second one.
- 77:19 The fault here is too much information. Length is not always the problem: unnecessary detail is.

*Wrong sentences corrected*

- 69:24 Mr Edwardson is a 29-year-old man who is single and works as a railway engineer and he was adopted at birth, so he has no knowledge of the medical history of his birth family, which means that his only past medical history is of certain childhood infections, none of which are unusual.
- 69:24 Mr Kozak has a history of excessive alcohol intake, which apparently started after the death of his mother, and it increases when he is anxious and has twice been diagnosed with depression for which he was prescribed citalopram, which he admits he never took regularly.
- 70:13 The ambulance team found Mrs Hardy, who was conscious but confused, after lying on the bedroom floor in her nightdress, at approximately 1.30pm after a call from her neighbour, Mr Bowen, who had become concerned that her curtains were still closed for some time without receiving an answer, and Mr Bowen also rang the patient's son, who arrived just before the ambulance and accompanied her to the hospital.
- 70:41 Mr Edwardson is a 29-year-old man who is single and works as a railway engineer and he was adopted at birth, so he has no knowledge of the medical history of his birth family, which means that his only past medical history is of certain childhood infections, none of which are unusual.

- 74:32 For which he was prescribed citalopram, which he admits he never took regularly.
- 76:27 The ambulance team found Mrs Hardy, who was conscious but confused, after lying on the bedroom floor in her nightdress, at approximately 1.30pm after a call from her neighbour, Mr Bowen, who had become concerned that her curtains were still closed for some time without receiving an answer, and Mr Bowen also rang the patient's son, who arrived just before the ambulance and accompanied her to the hospital.

*Terms*: Relative pronoun (Grammar word, 74:45); comply (verb), compliance (noun), non-compliance (noun) (Word family, 75:29); good compliance, poor compliance, with poor compliance (Collocations, 75:45)

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 24:10 A verb and its noun do not always come one after the other. If the noun is in the sentence, its verb can be further away.
- 24:55 Do not write 'assistant' when you mean 'assistance'. Many students mix these two words up in their writing.
- 26:03 Do not use 'due to' here. It makes the sentence wrong and the structure difficult.

## 3. Vocabulary for OET: Adjective + Noun Collocations

- Lesson ID and folder: `vocabulary-03-collocations-2` (folder `vocabulary-03-collocations-2`); course vocabulary, lesson 3; length 76:00; bundle format 1.22
- Status: approved by siavash, 2026-10-27 09:37
- Description: Learn adjectives and nouns that go together, and clear ways to describe pain, for your OET letters and for talking to patients.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 18:04 If you do not know the format, you can get confused in the exam. Confusion slows you down and costs marks.

*Terms*: Vocabulary bank (New phrase, 16:10)

### OET facts

- 2:39 (`reading-grade-b`) The target is Grade B, a score of 350.
- 4:34 (`reading-thirty-of-42`) About 30 of the 42 Reading questions correct usually gives Grade B; grade boundaries change a little between test sessions.
- 5:48 (`reading-timing`) Reading lasts 60 minutes: Part A has 15 minutes, timed on its own; Parts B and C share 45 minutes.
- 6:41 (`reading-part-a`) Part A has four short texts and 20 questions.
- 7:29 (`reading-part-b`) Part B has six short workplace texts of 100 to 150 words, one question each.
- 7:36 (`reading-part-c`) Part C has two long texts of about 800 words, with 16 questions of four options.
- 13:14 (`reading-one-mark`) Every question carries one mark.

*Rules on the boards*

- 21:37 The keywords in the question tell you which text to search. Go to that text only.
- 23:09 Always mark numbers while you skim. Questions are often asked about them.
- 28:15 Write the answer in the exact form the text gives. Do not change a verb and do not shorten a word.
- 42:36 In sentence completion, write exactly what the text has. If the text has a past form, write the past form.
- 48:02 The question often uses a different word from the text. Each keyword has an equivalent in the text, and finding it tells you that you are in the right place.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 22:06 Do not scan for several questions at the same time. One question, one search.
- 39:03 The idea is right, but the form is not. Copy the word as the text writes it.

*Terms*: Scanning (New word, 20:51); Peripheral vision (New phrase, 24:32)

### OET facts

- 0:12 (`reading-part-a`) Part A has four short texts and 20 questions.
- 8:24 (`reading-part-a-time-plan`) Part A time plan: three minutes to skim the four texts, then the questions in order (two minutes for the matching questions).
- 13:10 (`reading-pencils`) On the paper test, mark with a pen or a pencil; highlighters are not allowed.
- 53:40 (`reading-thirty-of-42`) About 30 of the 42 Reading questions correct usually gives Grade B; grade boundaries change a little between test sessions.
- 55:36 (`reading-part-a-guide`) 15 of the 20 Part A questions is a guide within the Grade B target.

### Exam methods and strategies

- 12:17 **Skimming**: Read all four texts quickly first, by their headings, bullet points, tables, bold and italics; the path is highlighted.
- 28:26 **The Part A question method**: For each question: the four texts as a map, the question and its keywords, try it first, rule out the texts that cannot hold the answer, then zoom into the one that does and find the answer in its exact form (ADR 026).

### OET facts

- 4:47 (`reading-timing`) Reading lasts 60 minutes: Part A has 15 minutes, timed on its own; Parts B and C share 45 minutes.
- 6:05 (`reading-part-b`) Part B has six short workplace texts of 100 to 150 words, one question each.
- 21:14 (`reading-shade`) On the paper test, Part B and C answers are shaded with a 2B pencil, and only a shaded circle counts.
- 56:16 (`reading-thirty-of-42`) About 30 of the 42 Reading questions correct usually gives Grade B; grade boundaries change a little between test sessions.

### Exam methods and strategies

- 11:23 **The QAT method**: Question, Answers, Text: read the question and its three options, with their keywords, before the text.
- 16:13 **The Part B question method**: The question and its keywords, the three options, try it first, then the extract: keyword pairs, the paraphrase of the right option, a reason for each wrong option, and the answer confirmed (ADR 026).

### Vocabulary

First moment each word is taught. Word-bank items carry their `lx:` ID; their meanings stay in the exercises repository.

- 8:06 Gist
- 13:14 cannot be excluded (`lx:cannot-be-excluded:phrase:still-a-possibility`)
- 13:43 postpone (`lx:postpone:verb:delay-to-later-time`)
- 14:08 outweigh (`lx:outweigh:verb:be-more-important-than`)
- 14:32 behind something (`lx:behind-something:phrase:responsible-for-something`)
- 14:57 establish (`lx:establish:verb:find-out-for-certain`)

## 4. Reading Part C (1)

- Description: How to read the two long Part C texts for the writer's meaning and opinion, and how to solve each question with the QTA method.

*Rules on the boards*

- 3:23 In Part C you read, think and reason. The answer is in the meaning, not in matching words.

*Terms*: Comprehension (New term, 2:05)

#### 3:50 Target score (`page-5`, deck)

- 3:50 **Target score for Reading Part C**
  - 3:50 Show the slide's own content: the Part C target

  - 5:20 Warn against relying on one part only
  - 5:34 Close with the goal: build skill in all three parts

*Rules on the boards*

- 5:08 Extra marks in Part A take some of the pressure off Part C.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 5:20 You cannot know what the texts will be like on test day.

#### 5:53 Part C: breakdown (`page-6`, deck)

- 5:53 **Part C breakdown table**
  - 5:53 introduce the breakdown of Part C
  - 6:14 how the 45 shared minutes are split
  - 6:35 where Part A sits in the timing
  - 6:57 how many texts

  - 7:40 the question often names its paragraph
  - 7:55 the working method
  - 8:19 number of options, against Part B
  - 8:28 marks

*Rules on the boards*

- 7:55 Work paragraph by paragraph: read the question, read the paragraph it points to, then answer.

#### 8:39 Critical thinking (`page-7`, deck)

- 8:39 **Critical thinking in Part C**
  - 8:39 Frame what Part C asks the reader to do, and name the skill
  - 9:19 Contrast stated meaning with meaning that follows logically
  - 9:56 Show what reading deeply gives you, and what the correct option usually does
  - 10:41 Gloss the hard general word 'abstract'
  - 11:04 First part of critical thinking: logical reasoning
  - 11:25 Second part: analysis
  - 11:53 Third part: engagement, rebuilt without the Persian idiom
  - 12:08 Pull the three parts together into the rule to remember

*Rules on the boards*

- 10:22 The correct answer is often the option that sums up what the text does not say directly.
- 12:08 Reason, analyse and engage with each paragraph. Then you can see the idea behind the writer's words.

*Terms*: Critical thinking (New term, 8:39)

#### 12:37 How to identify opinions (`page-8`, deck)

- 12:37 **opinion words and hedging words**
  - 12:37 Show the first two kinds of opinion signal and say why they matter
  - 13:05 What Part C texts are like, and how to work through a long sentence
  - 13:22 New words do not block the answer
  - 13:45 An opinion word inside a healthcare sentence
  - 14:11 Tell the writer's own view from a view the writer reports
  - 14:44 Define a hedging word
  - 15:03 Certain statement against a hedged one
  - 15:19 An option that drops the hedge is wrong
  - 15:50 Why this trap works
- 16:21 **emotion words and the main clause**
  - 16:21 Show the last two kinds of signal
  - 16:43 An emotion word in a healthcare sentence
  - 16:54 A second word of the same kind
  - 17:17 Split a long sentence into its two parts
  - 17:44 The completeness test, done in English
  - 18:06 Name the main clause and say what it holds
  - 18:21 Do not take the opinion from the other parts
  - 18:51 Attitude words that come back in the options
  - 19:10 Close the four signals

*Rules on the boards*

- 12:46 The answer is not copied word for word from the text. These signals help you rule out the wrong options and find the right one.
- 19:10 Look for these signals in the paragraph. They show you the writer's opinion, and the opinion gives you the answer.

*Warnings (common mistakes)*

- 15:50 An option that drops the hedge says more than the text says. It is wrong.
- 18:21 The other parts may say negative things. Do not use them to choose the writer's opinion.

*Terms*: Hedging word (New term, 14:44); Main clause (New term, 18:06)

#### 19:31 The QTA method (`page-9`, deck)

- 19:31 **QTA: the three steps for Part C**
  - 19:31 Put the three steps on the board and name the method
  - 20:03 Step one: the question stem only
  - 20:23 Contrast the order with Part B
  - 20:45 Why the order differs
  - 21:21 Step two: read the paragraph and mark key words
  - 21:28 Step two's aim: link the paragraph to the question
  - 21:46 Step three: go to the options and eliminate
  - 21:55 Teach the phrase 'rule out'
  - 22:06 Clinical example of 'rule out', and the meaning it does not have
  - 22:26 The same idea in the exam
  - 22:31 The last move: the best match

*Rules on the boards*

- 20:57 In Part C the paragraph is long, and the four options are written to look right. They reuse words from the text. Build your own understanding of the paragraph first.
- 21:28 Your aim is not only to read. It is to link the paragraph to the question you have just read, and to see its main idea.
- 22:26 In Part C, to rule out an option means to remove it.

*Terms*: QTA (Method, 19:34); Question stem (New words, 20:03); Rule out (New phrase, 21:55)

#### 92:46 Closing (`added-01`, closing)

- 92:46 **Closing: what to remember**
  - 92:46 The main point: read for meaning and opinion
  - 93:03 The method, as a path of steps
  - 93:24 Time, and a warm goodbye pointing to the next lesson

*Rules on the boards*

- 93:24 Give Part C about 35 minutes. That is a little over two minutes a question.

### OET facts

- 0:14 (`reading-part-c`) Part C has two long texts of about 800 words, with 16 questions of four options.
- 4:03 (`reading-thirty-of-42`) About 30 of the 42 Reading questions correct usually gives Grade B; grade boundaries change a little between test sessions.
- 4:34 (`reading-part-a-guide`) 15 of the 20 Part A questions is a guide within the Grade B target.
- 6:33 (`reading-timing`) Reading lasts 60 minutes: Part A has 15 minutes, timed on its own; Parts B and C share 45 minutes.
- 8:28 (`reading-one-mark`) Every question carries one mark.

### Exam methods and strategies

- 12:37 **Opinion signals**: A writer's view shows in four signals: opinion words, hedges, words of emotion or judgement, and the main clause that holds the view.
- 19:31 **The QTA method**: Question, Text, Answers: read the stem without the options, read the paragraph it points to and answer in your own words, then read the four options.
- 28:20 **The Part C question method**: In the order of the text, paragraph by paragraph: the question alone, the paragraph it is about, your own answer, the four options, try it first, the paraphrase of the right option, a reason for each wrong option, then the answer confirmed (ADR 026).
- 42:18 **The attitude scale**: For a question about a view: a line from negative to positive, a marker moved as the evidence is read, and each option placed on it.

  - 1:23 Set up the board: three steps for every Part C question

### OET facts

- 5:09 (`reading-timing`) Reading lasts 60 minutes: Part A has 15 minutes, timed on its own; Parts B and C share 45 minutes.
- 5:17 (`reading-part-c`) Part C has two long texts of about 800 words, with 16 questions of four options.
- 5:29 (`reading-shade`) On the paper test, Part B and C answers are shaded with a 2B pencil, and only a shaded circle counts.

### Exam methods and strategies

- 1:23 **The QTA method (review)**: Question, Text, Answers, in three steps, reviewed before the practice.
- 3:04 **Opinion signals (review)**: The four signals of a view, reviewed.
- 4:28 **The attitude scale (review)**: Rule out an option at the wrong point on the line, or one that goes too far.
- 8:58 **The Part C question method**: Applied to all eight questions of Text 2, paragraph by paragraph.

### OET facts

- 1:47 (fact 19) Speakers have a range of accents
- 2:03 (fact 6) Part A: two consultations between a health professional and a patient, about 4.5 to 5 minutes each, 12 gaps each, notes completed with a word or a short phrase
- 2:53 (fact 7) Part A: 30 seconds to look at the notes before each recording
- 3:06 (fact 11) Part B: six short workplace recordings (handovers, training sessions, briefings, colleagues talking, a patient and a health professional), one or two speakers, at least one a health professional, about 45 seconds each
- 3:36 (fact 12) Part B: one question per recording, with three options (A, B, C)
- 3:45 (fact 13) Part B: 15 seconds to read the question before each recording
- 4:18 (fact 14) Part C: two recordings, presentations or interviews (two of one kind or one of each), about 4 to 5 minutes each, six questions each with three options
- 4:30 (fact 15) Part C: 90 seconds to read the questions before each recording
- 5:05 (fact 1) Listening has three parts and 42 questions
- 5:05 (fact 3) The Listening test takes about 40 minutes
- 5:11 (fact 2) Each question is worth one mark: Part A 24 marks, Part B 6, Part C 12 (42 in all)
- 5:20 (fact 22) A target of about 20 of 24 in Part A (or 4 of 6 in Part B, 8 of 12 in Part C) *(the teacher's own recommendation, in the first person)*
- 5:38 (fact 18) Grade B (350) typically needs at least 30 of 42 marks; boundaries vary slightly by test version
- 6:00 (fact 5) Each part is heard once only
- 6:18 (fact 16) On paper, two minutes at the end to check all answers; on computer and OET@Home, a short pause after each question
- 6:42 (fact 10) Spelling matters in Listening: marks may be lost for spelling mistakes; UK and US spellings are accepted and minor variations may be accepted if the meaning is clear
- 6:59 (fact 17) Part A answers are written in the booklet; Parts B and C answers are shaded
- 6:59 (fact 23) Paper test: Part A may be written in pen or pencil; Parts B and C shaded with a 2B pencil
- 7:54 (fact 8) Part A: write the words you hear, without paraphrasing; marks are lost for repeating words from the notes, adding information not in the recording, contradicting yourself or being unclear *(write the words you hear)*
- 8:30 (fact 24) What Part B tests: the main idea (gist) of a workplace interaction, specific details, a speaker's purpose (why they say what they say), their attitude and opinion, and the listener's course of action (what they will do as a result) *(Part B: the gist)*
- 9:01 (fact 29) The speakers do not use exactly the words of the question sheet: listen for meaning, not just individual words
- 9:26 (fact 25) What Part C tests: following the logical flow of a longer presentation or interview, the gist (main idea), and attitude (how someone feels about something) and opinion (what they think about something) *(taught as the speaker's purpose and opinion)*
- 10:29 (fact 4) Listening is the same for every profession, on general healthcare topics

### OET facts

- 1:25 (fact 6) Part A: two consultations between a health professional and a patient, about 4.5 to 5 minutes each, 12 gaps each, notes completed with a word or a short phrase
- 1:59 (fact 5) Each part is heard once only
- 2:09 (fact 2) Each question is worth one mark: Part A 24 marks, Part B 6, Part C 12 (42 in all)
- 2:31 (fact 7) Part A: 30 seconds to look at the notes before each recording
- 5:08 (fact 16) On paper, two minutes at the end to check all answers; on computer and OET@Home, a short pause after each question
- 5:56 (fact 4) Listening is the same for every profession, on general healthcare topics
- 6:34 (fact 17) Part A answers are written in the booklet; Parts B and C answers are shaded
- 6:38 (fact 23) Paper test: Part A may be written in pen or pencil; Parts B and C shaded with a 2B pencil
- 7:00 (fact 8) Part A: write the words you hear, without paraphrasing; marks are lost for repeating words from the notes, adding information not in the recording, contradicting yourself or being unclear
- 7:19 (fact 9) Part A answers are generally one to three words; do not change the word form or add extra words
- 7:37 (fact 10) Spelling matters in Listening: marks may be lost for spelling mistakes; UK and US spellings are accepted and minor variations may be accepted if the meaning is clear
- 16:12 (fact 22) A target of about 20 of 24 in Part A (or 4 of 6 in Part B, 8 of 12 in Part C) *(the teacher's own recommendation, in the first person)*
- 16:20 (fact 18) Grade B (350) typically needs at least 30 of 42 marks; boundaries vary slightly by test version

### Exam methods and strategies

- 2:25 **Predict each gap**: In the 30 seconds before each extract, read ahead: work out the kind of word each gap needs from the words around it.
- 16:49 **The gap-by-gap preview**: Before each recording, the note form gap by gap: the kind of word that fits and example words that are never the answer, with a pause after each (ADR 033).

### Vocabulary

First moment each word is taught. Word-bank items carry their `lx:` ID; their meanings stay in the exercises repository.

### OET facts

- 1:37 (fact 11) Part B: six short workplace recordings (handovers, training sessions, briefings, colleagues talking, a patient and a health professional), one or two speakers, at least one a health professional, about 45 seconds each
- 1:55 (fact 12) Part B: one question per recording, with three options (A, B, C)
- 2:02 (fact 2) Each question is worth one mark: Part A 24 marks, Part B 6, Part C 12 (42 in all)
- 2:09 (fact 13) Part B: 15 seconds to read the question before each recording
- 3:03 (fact 29) The speakers do not use exactly the words of the question sheet: listen for meaning, not just individual words
- 3:08 (fact 24) What Part B tests: the main idea (gist) of a workplace interaction, specific details, a speaker's purpose (why they say what they say), their attitude and opinion, and the listener's course of action (what they will do as a result)
- 3:32 (fact 14) Part C: two recordings, presentations or interviews (two of one kind or one of each), about 4 to 5 minutes each, six questions each with three options *(mentioned: Part C is taught later)*
- 7:47 (fact 30) Linking words (for example although, but, though, however, so, because) can signal important information that affects the answer *(contrast words, for Part B)*
- 11:49 (fact 22) A target of about 20 of 24 in Part A (or 4 of 6 in Part B, 8 of 12 in Part C) *(the teacher's own recommendation, in the first person)*
- 11:53 (fact 18) Grade B (350) typically needs at least 30 of 42 marks; boundaries vary slightly by test version
- 12:20 (fact 17) Part A answers are written in the booklet; Parts B and C answers are shaded
- 12:20 (fact 23) Paper test: Part A may be written in pen or pencil; Parts B and C shaded with a 2B pencil
- 15:08 (fact 5) Each part is heard once only
- 37:52 (fact 19) Speakers have a range of accents
- 50:00 (fact 4) Listening is the same for every profession, on general healthcare topics

### Exam methods and strategies

- 3:46 **Main point, not detail**: A speaker may mention all three options: choose the speaker's main point.
- 5:03 **Before you listen**: Use the 15 seconds to read the question carefully and know what to listen for.
- 6:58 **Signal words**: While you listen, follow the signal words; after a contrast word such as 'however' comes the real point.
- 9:21 **Tempting wrong options**: The kinds of tempting wrong option, each tied to the practice questions that show it (ADR 035).
- 13:43 **The option-by-option preview**: Before the recording: the stem, then each option and what to listen for, with a pause after each; try it first; then listen (ADR 035).

### OET facts

- 1:38 (fact 14) Part C: two recordings, presentations or interviews (two of one kind or one of each), about 4 to 5 minutes each, six questions each with three options
- 1:45 (fact 12) Part B: one question per recording, with three options (A, B, C)
- 1:53 (fact 2) Each question is worth one mark: Part A 24 marks, Part B 6, Part C 12 (42 in all)
- 2:32 (fact 26) Part C recordings are like the talks heard for professional development: workplace presentations, personal accounts of case studies or projects, presentations or discussions about research, trials, case studies or case reviews
- 3:26 (fact 27) Part C questions are answered in order as the audio plays; OET's tip: read all the questions quickly first (to know how the audio is structured and when the speaker moves to the next question), then go back to the options as quickly as you can *(the questions follow the talk in order)*
- 4:03 (fact 29) The speakers do not use exactly the words of the question sheet: listen for meaning, not just individual words
- 4:59 (fact 25) What Part C tests: following the logical flow of a longer presentation or interview, the gist (main idea), and attitude (how someone feels about something) and opinion (what they think about something)
- 5:24 (fact 15) Part C: 90 seconds to read the questions before each recording
- 6:39 (fact 28) Speakers use language (signposting) to show they are changing topic or that the next question is coming; in an interview, it can be the interviewer's question
- 7:46 (fact 31) Keep listening even if you think you have heard the answer: words or ideas from all three options are often heard
- 8:30 (fact 30) Linking words (for example although, but, though, however, so, because) can signal important information that affects the answer
- 9:19 (fact 22) A target of about 20 of 24 in Part A (or 4 of 6 in Part B, 8 of 12 in Part C) *(the teacher's own recommendation, in the first person)*
- 9:24 (fact 18) Grade B (350) typically needs at least 30 of 42 marks; boundaries vary slightly by test version
- 11:32 (fact 5) Each part is heard once only

### Exam methods and strategies

- 6:39 **Signposts**: Signposts tell you that a new point, or the next question, is coming.
- 8:00 **Signal words**: Contrast words such as but, however, yet and nevertheless: the main point often comes after them.
- 9:36 **Read the six questions first**: In the 90 seconds, read all six questions, with the options covered, and underline their keywords (ADR 036).
- 19:45 **The Part C question method**: For each question: what to listen for in each option, try it first, then listen for the lead-in and rule out what is not said.

### OET facts

- 1:14 (fact 14) Part C: two recordings, presentations or interviews (two of one kind or one of each), about 4 to 5 minutes each, six questions each with three options
- 1:22 (fact 12) Part B: one question per recording, with three options (A, B, C)
- 1:22 (fact 2) Each question is worth one mark: Part A 24 marks, Part B 6, Part C 12 (42 in all)
- 1:40 (fact 22) A target of about 20 of 24 in Part A (or 4 of 6 in Part B, 8 of 12 in Part C) *(the teacher's own recommendation, in the first person)*
- 1:46 (fact 18) Grade B (350) typically needs at least 30 of 42 marks; boundaries vary slightly by test version
- 1:57 (fact 15) Part C: 90 seconds to read the questions before each recording
- 2:52 (fact 30) Linking words (for example although, but, though, however, so, because) can signal important information that affects the answer
- 3:01 (fact 29) The speakers do not use exactly the words of the question sheet: listen for meaning, not just individual words
- 3:08 (fact 31) Keep listening even if you think you have heard the answer: words or ideas from all three options are often heard
- 4:04 (fact 28) Speakers use language (signposting) to show they are changing topic or that the next question is coming; in an interview, it can be the interviewer's question *(in an interview, the interviewer's question)*
- 4:56 (fact 27) Part C questions are answered in order as the audio plays; OET's tip: read all the questions quickly first (to know how the audio is structured and when the speaker moves to the next question), then go back to the options as quickly as you can
- 7:33 (fact 5) Each part is heard once only

### Exam methods and strategies

- 1:10 **The Part C method (review)**: The format, the 90 seconds, signposts, linking words, other words, and keep listening, reviewed before the practice.
- 4:04 **The interviewer as a signpost**: In an interview the interviewer's question can show where the next part starts; the question's keywords decide (ADR 037).
- 4:40 **Read the six questions first**: In the 90 seconds, read the six questions with the options covered, and look for each one's keywords.

### OET facts

- 1:37 (`writing-six-criteria`) Six criteria assess the letter, the same for every profession (writing-facts 8).
- 2:38 (`writing-letter-types`) The letter is usually a referral, a discharge or a transfer letter; OET also names advice letters (writing-facts 3).
- 3:59 (`writing-two-assessors`) Two assessors mark the letter (writing-facts 10).
- 5:42 (`writing-one-task`) Writing has one task: a letter written from case notes, to another health professional or to a person who helps with the patient's care (writing-facts 1).
- 5:53 (`writing-score-scale`) Each sub-test is scored from 0 to 500 (writing-facts 12).
- 6:02 (`writing-grade-b`) Grade B is a score of 350 to 440 (writing-facts 12).
- 6:11 (`writing-ielts`) OET's own table compares grade B with IELTS 7.0 to 7.5 (writing-facts 13).
- 7:34 (`writing-profession`) The Writing task is specific to your profession: a nurse writes a Nursing task, a doctor a Medicine task (writing-facts 2).
- 7:53 (`writing-time`) Writing takes 45 minutes: 5 minutes of reading time, then 40 minutes of writing time (writing-facts 4).
- 7:59 (`writing-reading-only`) The 5 minutes of reading time are for reading only: no writing, underlining or circling (writing-facts 5).
- 8:13 (`writing-notes-during-writing`) During the 40 minutes you can look at the task and the case notes again at any time (writing-facts 6).
- 8:25 (`writing-style`) The style is clinical and factual, with polite language and no personal feelings (writing-facts 21).
- 8:38 (`writing-body-counted`) Only the body of the letter, its paragraphs, is counted (writing-facts 16).
- 8:57 (`writing-word-guide`) 180 to 200 words is a guide for the body, not a strict limit, with no automatic penalty (writing-facts 14).
- 9:07 (`writing-length-signals`) Much longer usually means something the reader does not need; much shorter usually means something important is missing (writing-facts 15).
- 14:19 (`writing-address`) Copy the reader's name and address from the task (writing-facts 24).
- 14:52 (`writing-re-line`) The Re line gives the patient's full name and date of birth (writing-facts 26).
- 17:31 (`writing-sign-off`) Sign with a job title, not your own name (writing-facts 25). *(a job title, such as Doctor)*
- 18:08 (`writing-computer-counter`) On computer, the word counter counts every word typed, not only the body (writing-facts 17).

### Exam methods and strategies

- 3:01 **Five steps towards successful writing**: Grammar, then vocabulary, then the assessment criteria, then the general Writing lessons, then practice on your own profession's tasks; and first, look at one or two sample tasks and their model letters.
- 9:23 **The teacher's word-count advice**: Ten or twelve words more or fewer do no harm; what matters is that the reader gets everything they need, nothing they do not, and less important points briefly.
- 13:03 **Relevant or not**: Keep the notes this reader needs, such as the patient's job when a cause at work is asked about; leave out the rest, such as hobbies.
- 13:47 **The three parts of the case-notes paper**: The setting (today's date, who you are, the patient), the case notes, and the Writing Task (letter type, reader, purpose).

### OET facts

- 0:32 (`writing-six-criteria`) Six criteria assess the letter, the same for every profession (writing-facts 8).
- 1:53 (`writing-two-assessors`) Two assessors mark the letter (writing-facts 10).
- 3:27 (`writing-language`) Language: grammar, vocabulary, spelling, punctuation and sentence structure that make the meaning clear (writing-facts 23).
- 4:19 (`writing-score-scale`) Each sub-test is scored from 0 to 500 (writing-facts 12).
- 4:27 (`writing-grade-b`) Grade B is a score of 350 to 440 (writing-facts 12).
- 4:32 (`writing-ielts`) OET's own table compares grade B with IELTS 7.0 to 7.5 (writing-facts 13).
- 5:02 (`writing-criteria-scores`) Purpose is scored out of 3; each of the other five criteria is scored out of 7, on its own (writing-facts 9).
- 5:27 (`writing-grade-b-profile`) For grade B, you need at least 2 out of 3 for Purpose and at least 5 out of 7 for each of the other five criteria (writing-facts 11).
- 8:42 (`writing-purpose`) Purpose: the reader sees at once why you are writing; the purpose is stated clearly at the start and expanded with more detail at the end of the letter (writing-facts 18, 30).
- 12:25 (`writing-content`) Content: what this reader needs to continue care, with nothing important missing and the case notes represented accurately (writing-facts 19).
- 16:00 (`writing-conciseness`) Conciseness and Clarity: only the information the reader needs, and the case summarised and presented clearly (writing-facts 20).
- 16:32 (`writing-body-counted`) Only the body of the letter, its paragraphs, is counted (writing-facts 16).
- 16:32 (`writing-word-guide`) 180 to 200 words is a guide for the body, not a strict limit, with no automatic penalty (writing-facts 14).
- 16:48 (`writing-length-signals`) Much longer usually means something the reader does not need; much shorter usually means something important is missing (writing-facts 15).
- 21:07 (`writing-style`) The style is clinical and factual, with polite language and no personal feelings (writing-facts 21).
- 24:28 (`writing-abbreviations`) Abbreviate for the reader and do not overdo it; abbreviations better known than their full form, such as MRI, BMI, CT and ECG, can be used (writing-facts 21, 38).
- 25:09 (`writing-address`) Copy the reader's name and address from the task (writing-facts 24).
- 25:25 (`writing-re-line`) The Re line gives the patient's full name and date of birth (writing-facts 26).
- 25:53 (`writing-organisation`) Organisation: one topic per paragraph, the most important information first; simply following the order of the case notes is a weakness (writing-facts 22, 40).
- 26:23 (`writing-sign-off`) Sign with a job title, not your own name (writing-facts 25). *(a job title, such as Doctor)*
- 26:39 (`writing-no-fixed-layout`) There is no one required layout for the letter (writing-facts 24).
- 28:40 (`writing-spelling`) Spelling does not need to be perfect for grade B (writing-facts 43).

### Exam methods and strategies

- 8:13 **Expanding the purpose (the teacher's method)**: Say briefly in the opening why you are writing and what you ask; expand it straight after, in the next paragraph; give the requests in detail at the end.
- 10:44 **Read the paper in this order**: The setting first, then the Writing Task, then the case notes line by line; ask who the reader is and what they will do.
- 16:41 **The teacher's word-count advice**: Ten or twelve words more or fewer do no harm; what matters is that the reader gets everything they need, nothing they do not, and less important points briefly.
- 17:45 **Keep, keep briefly or leave out**: For each note, ask what this reader will do with it: lines that only look related are left out; lines that explain the problem are kept briefly.
- 23:43 **The teacher's advice on style**: Never firstly, secondly or finally in the letter; fortunately or unfortunately at most once, and never about a patient's habits.

### OET facts

- 6:27 (`writing-no-fixed-layout`) There is no one required layout for the letter (writing-facts 24).
- 10:53 (`writing-letter-date`) The letter's date is the date the paper gives at the start ("Assume that today's date is ..."), never a date from the notes; above or below the reader's address, written in full (writing-facts 44).
- 14:25 (`writing-address`) Copy the reader's name and address from the task (writing-facts 24).
- 15:50 (`writing-unnamed-reader`) "Yours faithfully" goes with "Dear Sir or Madam" when the reader's name is not known (writing-facts 27).
- 16:34 (`writing-re-line`) The Re line gives the patient's full name and date of birth (writing-facts 26).
- 18:53 (`writing-purpose`) Purpose: the reader sees at once why you are writing; the purpose is stated clearly at the start and expanded with more detail at the end of the letter (writing-facts 18, 30).
- 20:14 (`writing-paragraphs`) One topic in each paragraph; there is no fixed number of paragraphs (writing-facts 40).
- 24:09 (`writing-content`) Content: what this reader needs to continue care, with nothing important missing and the case notes represented accurately (writing-facts 19).
- 24:50 (`writing-closing-sentence`) A closing sentence commonly thanks the reader or offers contact; "Thank you for ..." and "Please do not hesitate to contact me if you have any questions" are both acceptable, neither required (writing-facts 39; ADR 040 decision 5).
- 25:23 (`writing-sign-off`) Sign with a job title, not your own name (writing-facts 25). *(a job title, such as Nurse)*

### Exam methods and strategies

- 8:19 **Where each part comes from**: The date from the date line at the top of the paper; the reader's name, address and the purpose from the Writing Task; the Re line from the patient details; the body from the notes; the request from the plan.
- 14:25 **Setting out the address**: Copy it from the Writing Task exactly, one part on each line, from the person to the town, keeping every capital letter.
- 19:04 **Expanding the purpose (the teacher's method)**: Say briefly at the start why you are writing and what you ask; expand it straight after, in the next paragraph; give the requests in detail at the end.
- 22:40 **From the plan to the request**: Turn each line of the plan into a polite request, keeping its date, what to do and when; report what the patient said at its exact degree.

#### 31:22 Closing (`added-01`, closing)

- 31:22 **Closing**
  - 31:22 Recap

### OET facts

- 4:20 (`writing-unnamed-reader`) "Yours faithfully" goes with "Dear Sir or Madam" when the reader's name is not known (writing-facts 27).
- 5:05 (`writing-re-line`) The Re line gives the patient's full name and date of birth (writing-facts 26).
- 6:10 (`writing-fit-the-case`) Full marks for Purpose need writing fitted to the case, not learnt phrases (writing-facts 33).
- 16:51 (`writing-purpose`) Purpose: the reader sees at once why you are writing; the purpose is stated clearly at the start and expanded with more detail at the end of the letter (writing-facts 18, 30).
- 27:00 (`writing-opening-verbs`) "I am writing to refer" does not fit every letter: other verbs fit other tasks (writing-facts 32).

### Exam methods and strategies

- 6:01 **One clear opening sentence (the teacher's method)**: Put the patient, the problem and what you ask in one clear sentence, two at most, and fit it to each case.
- 16:59 **Expanding the purpose (the teacher's method)**: Say briefly in the opening what you ask; expand it straight after, in the next paragraph; give each request in full at the end, never the same sentence twice.
- 17:56 **Personal details in the opening (the teacher's method)**: A personal detail, such as the patient's job, goes into the opening only if this reader needs it.
- 22:10 **Time words counted from today (the teacher's method)**: Turn the paper's dates into words counted from the letter's date: today, this morning, since yesterday.
- 23:15 **Five questions to plan the opening (the teacher's method)**: Who, what, when, where and why, answered from the paper; one sentence or two.
